    uid=1_1652 "Python features "

    uid=1_1655 "iterable"
    uid=1_1656 " object on the right-hand side of the equal sign to produce the same number of values as the writable expressions on the left-hand side; while iterating, the statement assigns each of the values produced on the right to the corresponding expression on the left."

   uid=1_2281 region "Arithmetic operations"

    uid=1_2285 "Python includes conventional symbols for arithmetic operators ("
    uid=1_2286 "+"

    uid=1_2301 " e.g., "
    uid=1_2302 "4"
    uid=1_2303 " "
    uid=1_2304 "% -3 == -2"
    uid=1_2305 ".) Python also offers the "

    uid=1_2315 "@"
    uid=1_2316 "."
    uid=1_2317 link "[114]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-122"
    uid=1_2321 " These operators work as in traditional mathematics; with the same "
    uid=1_2322 link "precedence rules" description="Order of operations" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_operations"
    uid=1_2324 ", the "

    uid=1_2327 " operators "
    uid=1_2328 "+"
    uid=1_2329 " and "
    uid=1_2330 "-"

    uid=1_2332 link "unary" description="Unary operation" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unary_operation"
    uid=1_2334 ", to represent positive and negative numbers respectively."
    uid=1_2335 "Division between integers produces floating-point results. The behavior of division has changed significantly over time:"
    uid=1_2336 link "[115]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-pep0238-123"
    uid=1_2340 "The current version of Python (i.e., since 3.0) changed the "
    uid=1_2341 "/"
    uid=1_2342 " operator to always represent floating-point division, e.g., "
    uid=1_2343 "5"
    uid=1_2344 "/"
    uid=1_2345 "2"
    uid=1_2346 " "
    uid=1_2347 "=="
    uid=1_2348 " "
    uid=1_2349 "2.5"
    uid=1_2350 "."
    uid=1_2351 "The floor division "
    uid=1_2352 "//"
    uid=1_2353 " operator was introduced, meaning that "
    uid=1_2354 "7//3 == 2"
    uid=1_2355 ", "
    uid=1_2356 "-7//3 == -3"
    uid=1_2357 ", "
    uid=1_2358 "7.5//3 == 2.0"
    uid=1_2359 ", and "
    uid=1_2360 "-7.5//3 == -3.0"
    uid=1_2361 ". For Python 2.7, adding the "
    uid=1_2362 "from"
    uid=1_2363 " "
    uid=1_2364 "__future__"
    uid=1_2365 " "
    uid=1_2366 "import"
    uid=1_2367 " "
    uid=1_2368 "division"
    uid=1_2369 " statement allows a module in Python 2.7 to use Python 3.x rules for division (see above)."
    uid=1_2370 "In Python terms, the "
    uid=1_2371 "/"
    uid=1_2372 " operator represents "
    uid=1_2373 "true division"
    uid=1_2374 " (or simply "
    uid=1_2375 "division"
    uid=1_2376 "), while the "
    uid=1_2377 "//"
    uid=1_2378 " operator represents "
    uid=1_2379 "floor division."
    uid=1_2380 " Before version 3.0, the "
    uid=1_2381 "/"
    uid=1_2382 " operator represents "
    uid=1_2383 "classic division"
    uid=1_2384 "."
    uid=1_2385 link "[115]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-pep0238-123"
    uid=1_2389 link "Rounding" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rounding"
    uid=1_2391 " towards negative infinity, though a different method than in most languages, adds consistency to Python. For instance, this rounding implies that the equation "
    uid=1_2392 "("
    uid=1_2393 "a"
    uid=1_2394 " "
    uid=1_2395 "+"
    uid=1_2396 " "
    uid=1_2397 "b"

    uid=1_2405 "//"

    uid=1_2407 " "
    uid=1_2408 "+"
    uid=1_2409 " "
    uid=1_2410 "1"
    uid=1_2411 " is always true. Also, the rounding implies that the equation "
    uid=1_2412 "b"
    uid=1_2413 "*"
    uid=1_2414 "("
    uid=1_2415 "a"
    uid=1_2416 "//"
    uid=1_2417 "b"
    uid=1_2418 ")"
    uid=1_2419 " "
    uid=1_2420 "+"
    uid=1_2421 " "
    uid=1_2422 "a"

    uid=1_2425 " "

    uid=1_2427 " "

    uid=1_2430 "a"
    uid=1_2431 ". As expected, the result of "

    uid=1_2433 " lies in the "
    uid=1_2434 link "half-open interval" description="Half-open interval" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-open_interval"
    uid=1_2436 " [0, "

    uid=1_2441 "b"
    uid=1_2442 ", 0] when "
    uid=1_2443 "b"
    uid=1_2444 " is negative."
    uid=1_2445 link "[116]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-AutoNT-62-124"

  uid=1_2703 region "Libraries"
   uid=1_2704 heading "Libraries" level="2"
   uid=1_2705 link "edit" description="Edit section: Libraries" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Python_(programming_language)&action=edit&section=12"
   uid=1_2707 "Python's large standard library"
   uid=1_2708 link "[125]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-AutoNT-86-133"
   uid=1_2712 " is commonly cited as one of its greatest strengths. For Internet-facing applications, many standard formats and protocols such as "
   uid=1_2713 link "MIME" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIME"
   uid=1_2715 " and "
   uid=1_2716 link "HTTP" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HTTP"
   uid=1_2718 " are supported. The language includes modules for creating "
   uid=1_2719 link "graphical user interfaces" description="Graphical user interface" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graphical_user_interface"
   uid=1_2721 ", connecting to "
   uid=1_2722 link "relational databases" description="Relational database" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relational_database"
   uid=1_2724 ", "
   uid=1_2725 link "generating pseudorandom numbers" description="Pseudorandom number generator" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudorandom_number_generator"
   uid=1_2727 ", arithmetic with arbitrary-precision decimals,"
   uid=1_2728 link "[121]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-AutoNT-88-129"
   uid=1_2732 " manipulating "
   uid=1_2733 link "regular expressions" description="Regular expression" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_expression"
   uid=1_2735 ", and "
   uid=1_2736 link "unit testing" description="Unit testing" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_testing"
   uid=1_2738 "."
   uid=1_2739 "Some parts of the standard library are covered by specifications—for example, the "

    uid=1_3230 "Brython"

    uid=1_3250 " compiles a superset of Python"
    uid=1_3251 " to C. The resulting code can be used with Python via direct C-level API calls into the Python interpreter."
    uid=1_3252 "PyJL compiles/transpiles a subset of Python to "human-readable, maintainable, and high-performance Julia source code"."
    uid=1_3253 link "[76]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-PyJL-79"
    uid=1_3257 " Despite the developers' performance claims, this is not possible for "
    uid=1_3258 "arbitrary"
    uid=1_3259 " Python code; that is, compiling to a faster language or machine code is known to be impossible in the general case. The semantics of Python might potentially be changed, but in many cases speedup is possible with few or no changes in the Python code. The faster Julia source code can then be used from Python or compiled to machine code."
    uid=1_3260 link "Nuitka" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuitka"

   uid=1_3372 region "Performance"
    uid=1_3373 heading "Performance" level="3"
    uid=1_3374 link "edit" description="Edit section: Performance" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Python_(programming_language)&action=edit&section=19"
    uid=1_3376 "A performance comparison among various Python implementations, using a non-numerical (combinatorial) workload, was presented at EuroSciPy '13."
    uid=1_3377 link "[177]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-185"
    uid=1_3381 " In addition, Python's performance relative to other programming languages is benchmarked by "
    uid=1_3382 link "The Computer Language Benchmarks Game" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Computer_Language_Benchmarks_Game"
    uid=1_3384 "."
    uid=1_3385 link "[178]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-186"
    uid=1_3389 "There are several approaches to optimizing Python performance, despite the inherent slowness of an "
    uid=1_3390 link "interpreted language" description="Interpreted language" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpreted_language"
    uid=1_3392 ". These approaches include the following strategies or tools:"
    uid=1_3393 link "Just-in-time compilation" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Just-in-time_compilation"
    uid=1_3395 ": Dynamically compiling parts of a Python program during the execution of the program. This technique is used in libraries such as "
    uid=1_3396 link "Numba" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numba"
    uid=1_3398 " and "
    uid=1_3399 link "PyPy" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PyPy"
    uid=1_3401 "."
    uid=1_3402 link "Static compilation" description="Compiler" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compiler"
    uid=1_3404 ": Sometimes, Python code can be compiled into machine code sometime before execution. An example of this approach is "
    uid=1_3405 link "Cython" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cython"
    uid=1_3407 ", which compiles Python into C."
    uid=1_3408 link "Concurrency" description="Concurrent computing" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concurrent_computing"
    uid=1_3410 " and "
    uid=1_3411 link "parallelism" description="Parallel computing" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_computing"
    uid=1_3413 ": Multiple tasks can be run simultaneously. Python contains modules such as `multiprocessing` to support this form of parallelism. Moreover, this approach helps to overcome limitations of the "
    uid=1_3414 link "Global Interpreter Lock" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Interpreter_Lock"
    uid=1_3416 " (GIL) in CPU tasks."
    uid=1_3417 "Efficient "
    uid=1_3418 link "data structures" description="Data structures" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_structures"
    uid=1_3420 ": Performance can also be improved by using data types such as "
    uid=1_3421 "Set"
    uid=1_3422 " for membership tests, or "
    uid=1_3423 "deque"
    uid=1_3424 " from "
    uid=1_3425 "collections"
    uid=1_3426 " for "
    uid=1_3427 link "queue" description="Queueing theory" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queueing_theory"
    uid=1_3429 " operations."
    uid=1_3430 "Performance gains can be observed by utilizing libraries such as "
    uid=1_3431 link "NumPy" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NumPy"
    uid=1_3433 ". Most high-performance Python libraries use "
    uid=1_3434 link "C" description="C (programming language)" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C_(programming_language)"
    uid=1_3436 " or "
    uid=1_3437 link "Fortran" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortran"
    uid=1_3439 " under the hood instead of the Python interpreter."
    uid=1_3440 link "[179]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-187"

   uid=1_3497 "CPython's public releases have three types, distinguished by which part of the version number is incremented:"
   uid=1_3498 "Backward-incompatible versions"
   uid=1_3499 ", where code is expected to break and must be manually "
   uid=1_3500 link "ported" description="Ported" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ported"
   uid=1_3502 ". The first part of the version number is incremented. These releases happen infrequently—version 3.0 was released 8 years after 2.0. According to Guido van Rossum, a version 4.0 will probably never exist."
   uid=1_3503 link "[184]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-192"
   uid=1_3507 "Major or "feature" releases"
   uid=1_3508 " are largely compatible with the previous version but introduce new features. The second part of the version number is incremented. Starting with Python"
   uid=1_3509 " "
   uid=1_3510 "3.9, these releases are expected to occur annually."
   uid=1_3511 link "[185]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-193"
   uid=1_3515 link "[186]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-194"
   uid=1_3519 " Each major version is supported by bug fixes for several years after its release."
   uid=1_3520 link "[187]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-release-schedule-195"
   uid=1_3524 "Bug fix releases"
   uid=1_3525 ","
   uid=1_3526 link "[188]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-AutoNT-22-196"
   uid=1_3530 " which introduce no new features, occur approximately every three months; these releases are made when a sufficient number of bugs have been fixed "
   uid=1_3531 link "upstream" description="Upstream (software development)" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upstream_(software_development)"
   uid=1_3533 " since the last release. Security vulnerabilities are also patched in these releases. The third and final part of the version number is incremented."
   uid=1_3534 link "[188]" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Python_(programming_language)#cite_note-AutoNT-22-196"
   uid=1_3538 "Many "
   uid=1_3539 link "alpha, beta, and release-candidates" description="Beta release" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta_release"
   uid=1_3541 " are also released as previews and for testing before final releases. Although there is a rough schedule for releases, they are often delayed if the code is not ready yet. Python's development team monitors the state of the code by running a large "
   uid=1_3542 link "unit test" description="Unit test" url="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_test"
   uid=1_3544 " suite during development."

   uid=1_535 """
   uid=1_6939 "Mojo as a member of the Python family [..] Embracing Python massively simplifies our design efforts, because most of the syntax is already specified. [..] we decided that the right long-term goal for Mojo is to provide a superset of Python (i.e. be compatible with existing programs) and to embrace the CPython immediately for long-tail ecosystem enablement. To a Python programmer, we expect and hope that Mojo will be immediately familiar, while also providing new tools for developing systems-level code that enable you to do things that Python falls back to C and C++ for."
   uid=1_535 """
